⇓ e \Downarrow_{e} -Ite-E2
Figure 25 . Expression semantics (error rules)
There are a variety of wrong behaviors prevented by our type system,
mostly concerning mismatches between values and elimination forms:
unbound variables ( ⇓ e \Downarrow_{e} -Var-E );
mistyped arguments to built-in operations ( ⇓ e \Downarrow_{e} -Op-E2 );
function, relation and constructor arity errors
( ⇓ e \Downarrow_{e} -Fun-E2 , ⇓ e \Downarrow_{e} -Rel-E2 , ⇓ e \Downarrow_{e} -Match-E4 );
non-existent functions and relations
( ⇓ e \Downarrow_{e} -Fun-E3 , ⇓ e \Downarrow_{e} -Rel-E3 );
conditionals on inappropriate values
( ⇓ e \Downarrow_{e} -Match-E2 , ⇓ e \Downarrow_{e} -Ite-E2 );
and ill formed constructor names ( ⇓ e \Downarrow_{e} -Match-E3 );
The remaining rules propagate errors
( ⇓ e \Downarrow_{e} -Let-E , ⇓ e \Downarrow_{e} -Op-E1 , ⇓ e \Downarrow_{e} -Fun-E1 , ⇓ e \Downarrow_{e} -Rel-E1 , ⇓ e \Downarrow_{e} -Match-E1 , ⇓ e \Downarrow_{e} -Ite-E1 ).
As mentioned in the early discussion of our semantics in this
section, ⇓ e \Downarrow_{e} -Op-E2 is not about division by zero
(a form of going wrong our type system doesn’t prevent), but
about mis-application of built-in functions, e.g., taking the boolean
negation of a number.
Formula semantics
  F → ; 𝒲 ; θ ⊢ ϕ ⇓ ϕ v ⊥ {\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\vec{F};{}}}\mathcal{W};\theta\vdash\phi\Downarrow_{\phi}v_{\bot}
  F → ; 𝒲 ; θ ⊢ ϕ → ⇓ ϕ → v → ⊥ {\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\vec{F};{}}}\mathcal{W};\theta\vdash\vec{\phi}\Downarrow_{\vec{\phi}}\vec{v}_{\bot}
𝒲 ; θ ⊢ ⋅ ⇓ ϕ → ⋅ \displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\end{array}}{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\mathcal{W};\theta\vdash\cdot\Downarrow_{\vec{\phi}}\cdot\end{array}}   ⇓ ϕ → \Downarrow_{\vec{\phi}} -Empty
𝒲 ; θ ⊢ ϕ ⇓ ϕ v 𝒲 ; θ ⊢ ϕ i → ⇓ ϕ → v i → 𝒲 ; θ ⊢ ϕ , ϕ i → ⇓ ϕ → v , v i → \displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\mathcal{W};\theta\vdash\phi\Downarrow_{\phi}v\quad\quad\mathcal{W};\theta\vdash\vec{\phi_{i}}\Downarrow_{\vec{\phi}}\vec{v_{i}}\end{array}}{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\mathcal{W};\theta\vdash\phi,\vec{\phi_{i}}\Downarrow_{\vec{\phi}}v,\vec{v_{i}}\end{array}}
⇓ ϕ → \Downarrow_{\vec{\phi}} -All
𝒲 ; θ ⊢ ϕ i → ⇓ ϕ → v i → 𝒲 ; θ ⊢ ϕ ⇓ ϕ ⊥ 𝒲 ; θ ⊢ ϕ i → , ϕ , ϕ j → ⇓ ϕ → ⊥ \displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\mathcal{W};\theta\vdash\vec{\phi_{i}}\Downarrow_{\vec{\phi}}\vec{v_{i}}\quad\quad\mathcal{W};\theta\vdash\phi\Downarrow_{\phi}\bot\end{array}}{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\mathcal{W};\theta\vdash\vec{\phi_{i}},\phi,\vec{\phi_{j}}\Downarrow_{\vec{\phi}}\bot\end{array}} ⇓ ϕ → \Downarrow_{\vec{\phi}} -All-E
𝒲 ; θ ⊢ e ⇓ e v 𝒲 ; θ ⊢ , e ⇓ ϕ toSMT ( v ) \displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\mathcal{W};\theta\vdash e\Downarrow_{e}v\end{array}}{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\mathcal{W};\theta\vdash\textbf{,}{e}\Downarrow_{\phi}\mathrm{toSMT}(v)\end{array}}
⇓ ϕ \Downarrow_{\phi} -Unquote
𝒲 ; θ ⊢ e ⇓ e ⊥ 𝒲 ; θ ⊢ , e ⇓ ϕ ⊥ \displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\mathcal{W};\theta\vdash e\Downarrow_{e}\bot\end{array}}{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\mathcal{W};\theta\vdash\textbf{,}{e}\Downarrow_{\phi}\bot\end{array}}
⇓ ϕ \Downarrow_{\phi} -Unquote-E
𝒲 ; θ ⊢ ϕ i → ⇓ ϕ → v i → 𝒲 ; θ ⊢ c 𝖲𝖬𝖳 𝖼 ( ϕ → ) ⇓ ϕ c 𝖲𝖬𝖳 𝖼 ( v → ) \displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\mathcal{W};\theta\vdash\vec{\phi_{i}}\Downarrow_{\vec{\phi}}\vec{v_{i}}\end{array}}{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\mathcal{W};\theta\vdash c^{\mathsf{SMT}}_{\mathsf{c}}(\vec{\phi})\Downarrow_{\phi}c^{\mathsf{SMT}}_{\mathsf{c}}(\vec{v})\end{array}}
⇓ ϕ \Downarrow_{\phi} -Ctor
𝒲 ; θ ⊢ ϕ i → ⇓ ϕ → ⊥ 𝒲 ; θ ⊢ c 𝖲𝖬𝖳 𝖼 ( ϕ i → ) ⇓ ϕ ⊥ \displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\mathcal{W};\theta\vdash\vec{\phi_{i}}\Downarrow_{\vec{\phi}}\bot\end{array}}{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\mathcal{W};\theta\vdash c^{\mathsf{SMT}}_{\mathsf{c}}(\vec{\phi_{i}})\Downarrow_{\phi}\bot\end{array}}
⇓ ϕ \Downarrow_{\phi} -Ctor-E
𝒲 ; θ ⊢ c 𝖲𝖬𝖳 𝖼 ( v i → ) ⇓ ϕ c 𝖲𝖬𝖳 𝖼 ( v i → ) \displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\end{array}}{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\mathcal{W};\theta\vdash c^{\mathsf{SMT}}_{\mathsf{c}}(\vec{v_{i}})\Downarrow_{\phi}c^{\mathsf{SMT}}_{\mathsf{c}}(\vec{v_{i}})\end{array}} ⇓ ϕ \Downarrow_{\phi} -SMT-Value
SMT conversion
  toSMT ⁡ ( v ) = v \mathrm{toSMT}(v)=v
toSMT ⁡ ( k ) = c 𝖼𝗈𝗇𝗌𝗍 𝖲𝖬𝖳 ​ [ k ] ​ ( ) toSMT ⁡ ( c ⁡ ( v i → ) ) = c 𝖼𝗍𝗈𝗋 𝖲𝖬𝖳 ​ [ c ] ​ ( toSMT ⁡ ( v i ) → ) toSMT ⁡ ( c 𝖼𝗈𝗇𝗌𝗍 𝖲𝖬𝖳 ​ [ k ] ​ ( ) ) = c 𝖼𝗈𝗇𝗌𝗍 𝖲𝖬𝖳 ​ [ k ] ​ ( ) toSMT ⁡ ( c 𝗏𝖺𝗋 𝖲𝖬𝖳 ​ [ x , t ] ​ ( ) ) = c 𝗏𝖺𝗋 𝖲𝖬𝖳 ​ [ x , t ] ​ ( ) \begin{array}[]{@{}rcl@{}}\mathrm{toSMT}(k)&=&c^{\mathsf{SMT}}_{\mathsf{const}}[k]()\\
\mathrm{toSMT}(c(\vec{v_{i}}))&=&c^{\mathsf{SMT}}_{\mathsf{ctor}}[c](\overrightarrow{\mathrm{toSMT}(v_{i})})\\
\mathrm{toSMT}(c^{\mathsf{SMT}}_{\mathsf{const}}[k]())&=&c^{\mathsf{SMT}}_{\mathsf{const}}[k]()\\[3.50006pt]
\mathrm{toSMT}(c^{\mathsf{SMT}}_{\mathsf{var}}[x,t]())&=&c^{\mathsf{SMT}}_{\mathsf{var}}[x,t]()\\[3.50006pt]
\end{array}
toSMT ⁡ ( c 𝖼𝗍𝗈𝗋 𝖲𝖬𝖳 ​ [ c ] ​ ( v i → ) ) = c 𝖼𝗍𝗈𝗋 𝖲𝖬𝖳 ​ [ c ] ​ ( v i → ) toSMT ⁡ ( c 𝗅𝖾𝗍 𝖲𝖬𝖳 ​ ( v 1 , v 2 , v 3 ) ) = c 𝗅𝖾𝗍 𝖲𝖬𝖳 ​ ( v 1 , v 2 , v 3 ) toSMT ⁡ ( c 𝖿𝗈𝗋𝖺𝗅𝗅 𝖲𝖬𝖳 ​ ( v 1 , v 2 ) ) = c 𝖿𝗈𝗋𝖺𝗅𝗅 𝖲𝖬𝖳 ​ ( v 1 , v 2 ) toSMT ⁡ ( c 𝗎𝖿 𝖲𝖬𝖳 ​ [ 𝑢𝑓 ] ​ ( v i → ) ) = c 𝗎𝖿 𝖲𝖬𝖳 ​ [ 𝑢𝑓 ] ​ ( v i → ) \begin{array}[]{@{}rcl@{}}\mathrm{toSMT}(c^{\mathsf{SMT}}_{\mathsf{ctor}}[c](\vec{v_{i}}))&=&c^{\mathsf{SMT}}_{\mathsf{ctor}}[c](\vec{v_{i}})\\[3.50006pt]
\mathrm{toSMT}(c^{\mathsf{SMT}}_{\mathsf{let}}(v_{1},v_{2},v_{3}))&=&c^{\mathsf{SMT}}_{\mathsf{let}}(v_{1},v_{2},v_{3})\\[3.50006pt]
\mathrm{toSMT}(c^{\mathsf{SMT}}_{\mathsf{forall}}(v_{1},v_{2}))&=&c^{\mathsf{SMT}}_{\mathsf{forall}}(v_{1},v_{2})\\[3.50006pt]
\mathrm{toSMT}(c^{\mathsf{SMT}}_{\mathsf{uf}}[\mathit{uf}](\vec{v_{i}}))&=&c^{\mathsf{SMT}}_{\mathsf{uf}}[\mathit{uf}](\vec{v_{i}})\\[3.50006pt]
\end{array}
Figure 26 . Formula semantics
The operational semantics on formulas is simple: the rules generate
ASTs for the SMT solver using the c … 𝖲𝖬𝖳 c^{\mathsf{SMT}}_{\mathsf{\dots}} constructors:
constants ( c 𝖼𝗈𝗇𝗌𝗍 𝖲𝖬𝖳 c^{\mathsf{SMT}}_{\mathsf{const}} ), SMT variables ( c 𝗏𝖺𝗋 𝖲𝖬𝖳 c^{\mathsf{SMT}}_{\mathsf{var}} ), SMT data types ( c 𝖼𝗍𝗈𝗋 𝖲𝖬𝖳 c^{\mathsf{SMT}}_{\mathsf{ctor}} ),
let bindings ( c 𝗅𝖾𝗍 𝖲𝖬𝖳 c^{\mathsf{SMT}}_{\mathsf{let}} ), quantification ( c 𝖿𝗈𝗋𝖺𝗅𝗅 𝖲𝖬𝖳 c^{\mathsf{SMT}}_{\mathsf{forall}} ), and uninterpreted
function application ( c 𝗎𝖿 𝖲𝖬𝖳 c^{\mathsf{SMT}}_{\mathsf{uf}} ).
When unquoting values resulting from evaluating expressions, we use
the toSMT \mathrm{toSMT} function to translate expression values into the SMT’s
AST.
The toSMT \mathrm{toSMT} function is an identity on SMT ASTs, but it explicitly
tags the constants and Formulog-defined constructors using c 𝖼𝗈𝗇𝗌𝗍 𝖲𝖬𝖳 c^{\mathsf{SMT}}_{\mathsf{const}} and
c 𝖼𝗍𝗈𝗋 𝖲𝖬𝖳 c^{\mathsf{SMT}}_{\mathsf{ctor}} .
Appendix D Metatheory
We break the metatheory into two parts: lemmas characterizing the SMT
conversion (Section D.1 ) and lemmas showing type
safety (Section E ).
The SMT lemmas culminate in two proofs:
first, regularity (Lemma D.9 ) guarantees that (a) every type
or context generated by the operational semantics is well formed and
(b) that formula evaluation generates well typed SMT ASTs;
second, we show that SMT conversion of values agrees with SMT
conversion of types (Lemma D.10 ).
Type safety culminates in theorems showing that premises don’t yield
⊥ \bot and generate well typed substitutions
(Lemma E.4 ) and so Horn clauses (a) never yield
⊥ \bot (Theorem E.6 ) and (b) take well
typed worlds to well typed worlds
(Theorem E.7 ).
D.1. SMT Conversion
We show a variety of properties of the erasure and SMT conversion
functions:
𝗌𝗆𝗍 \mathsf{smt} is a sub-kind of 𝖾𝗑𝗉 \mathsf{exp} (Lemma D.1 );
erasures and SMT conversion yield well formed types from SMT types
(Lemmas D.2 , D.3 , D.4 ,
and D.5 );
weakening and strengthening of typing contexts (Lemmas D.6 and D.7 );
type variable substitution (Lemma D.8 )—we have no
need of a value substitution lemma because our semantics uses
environments;
regularity (Lemma D.9 );
and, finally, that SMT conversion of values agrees with SMT conversion
of types (Lemma D.10 ).
Lemma D.1 ( 𝗌𝗆𝗍 \mathsf{smt} is a subkind of 𝖾𝗑𝗉 \mathsf{exp} ).
If Γ ⊢ 𝗌𝗆𝗍 τ \Gamma\vdash_{\mathsf{smt}}\tau then Γ ⊢ 𝖾𝗑𝗉 τ \Gamma\vdash_{\mathsf{exp}}\tau .
Proof.
By induction on τ \tau .
( τ = B \tau=B )
Immediate: τ \tau -Base allows any m m .
( τ = α \tau=\alpha )
Contradictory—type variables are only well formed at 𝖾𝗑𝗉 \mathsf{exp} .
( τ = D ​ τ i → \tau=D~\vec{\tau_{i}} )
By the IH on each τ i \tau_{i} and t t -ADT .
( τ = t ​ 𝗌𝗆𝗍 \tau={t}~\mathsf{smt} )
Immediate: τ \tau -SMT allows any m m .
( τ = t ​ 𝗌𝗒𝗆 \tau={t}~\mathsf{sym} )
Immediate: τ \tau -SMT allows any m m .
( τ = 𝗆𝗈𝖽𝖾𝗅 \tau=\mathsf{model} )
Contradictory— 𝗆𝗈𝖽𝖾𝗅 \mathsf{model} is only well formed at 𝖾𝗑𝗉 \mathsf{exp} .
∎
Lemma D.2 (Erasure is well formed).
If Γ ⊢ 𝗌𝗆𝗍 τ \Gamma\vdash_{\mathsf{smt}}\tau then Γ ⊢ 𝗌𝗆𝗍 erase ( τ ) \Gamma\vdash_{\mathsf{smt}}\mathrm{erase}(\tau) .
Proof.
By induction on the well formedness derivation.
( t t -Base )
Immediate, since erase ⁡ ( B ) = B \mathrm{erase}(B)=B .
( t t -TVar )
Contradictory—type variables aren’t well typed at 𝗌𝗆𝗍 \mathsf{smt} .
( t t -ADT )
By the IH on each constituent of D ​ τ i → D~\vec{\tau_{i}} , and then by t t -ADT .
( τ \tau -SMT )
Since erase ⁡ ( t ​ 𝗌𝗆𝗍 ) = erase ⁡ ( t ) \mathrm{erase}({t}~\mathsf{smt})=\mathrm{erase}(t) , by the
IH on Γ ⊢ 𝗌𝗆𝗍 t \Gamma\vdash_{\mathsf{smt}}t .
( τ \tau -Sym )
Since erase ⁡ ( t ​ 𝗌𝗒𝗆 ) = erase ⁡ ( t ) \mathrm{erase}({t}~\mathsf{sym})=\mathrm{erase}(t) , by the
IH on Γ ⊢ 𝗌𝗆𝗍 t \Gamma\vdash_{\mathsf{smt}}t .
( τ \tau -Model )
Contradictory— 𝗆𝗈𝖽𝖾𝗅 \mathsf{model} isn’t well typed
at 𝗌𝗆𝗍 \mathsf{smt} .
∎
Lemma D.3 (SMT types have only SMT parts).
If Γ ⊢ 𝗌𝗆𝗍 τ \Gamma\vdash_{\mathsf{smt}}\tau , then all of τ \tau ’s subparts are also well formed at 𝗌𝗆𝗍 \mathsf{smt} .
Proof.
By induction on τ \tau .
( τ = B \tau=B )
Immediate.
( τ = α \tau=\alpha )
Contradictory—type variables are only well formed at 𝖾𝗑𝗉 \mathsf{exp} .
( τ = D ​ τ i → \tau=D~\vec{\tau_{i}} )
By the IH on each τ i \tau_{i} .
( τ = t ​ 𝗌𝗆𝗍 \tau={t}~\mathsf{smt} )
By the IH on t t .
( τ = t ​ 𝗌𝗒𝗆 \tau={t}~\mathsf{sym} )
By the IH on t t .
( τ = 𝗆𝗈𝖽𝖾𝗅 \tau=\mathsf{model} )
Contradictory— 𝗆𝗈𝖽𝖾𝗅 \mathsf{model} is only well formed at 𝖾𝗑𝗉 \mathsf{exp} .
∎
Lemma D.4 (SMT conversion is well formed).
If Γ ⊢ 𝗌𝗆𝗍 τ \Gamma\vdash_{\mathsf{smt}}\tau then toSMT ⁡ ( τ ) = t ​ 𝗌𝗒𝗆 \mathrm{toSMT}(\tau)={t}~\mathsf{sym} or t ​ 𝗌𝗆𝗍 {t}~\mathsf{smt}
such that Γ ⊢ 𝗌𝗆𝗍 t \Gamma\vdash_{\mathsf{smt}}t (and so
Γ ⊢ 𝗌𝗆𝗍 toSMT ( τ ) \Gamma\vdash_{\mathsf{smt}}\mathrm{toSMT}(\tau) ).
Proof.
By induction on the well formedness derivation.
( t t -Base )
toSMT ⁡ ( B ) = B ​ 𝗌𝗆𝗍 \mathrm{toSMT}(B)={B}~\mathsf{smt} , which is well formed
by τ \tau -SMT and t t -B .
( t t -TVar )
Contradictory—type variables are only well formed at 𝖾𝗑𝗉 \mathsf{exp} .
( t t -ADT )
We know that erase ⁡ ( D ​ τ i → ) \mathrm{erase}(D~\vec{\tau_{i}}) is
still well formed by Lemma D.2 ; then
by τ \tau -SMT .
( τ \tau -SMT )
Since it must be that Γ ⊢ 𝗌𝗆𝗍 t \Gamma\vdash_{\mathsf{smt}}t , then erase ⁡ ( t ) \mathrm{erase}(t) is also well formed by
Lemma D.2 ; then by τ \tau -SMT .
( τ \tau -Sym )
Since it must be that Γ ⊢ 𝗌𝗆𝗍 t \Gamma\vdash_{\mathsf{smt}}t , then erase ⁡ ( t ) \mathrm{erase}(t) is also well formed by
Lemma D.2 ; then by τ \tau -Sym .
( τ \tau -Model )
Contradictory— 𝗆𝗈𝖽𝖾𝗅 \mathsf{model} is only well formed at 𝗌𝗆𝗍 \mathsf{smt}
∎
Lemma D.5 (SMT conversion is only for SMT types).
toSMT ⁡ ( τ ) \mathrm{toSMT}(\tau) is defined iff Γ ⊢ 𝗌𝗆𝗍 τ \Gamma\vdash_{\mathsf{smt}}\tau .
Proof.
The right-to-left direction is proved by Lemma D.4 .
For left-to-right, we go by induction on τ \tau .
( τ = B \tau=B )
By t t -Base .
( τ = α \tau=\alpha )
Contradictory—type variables are undefined for erase \mathrm{erase} .
( τ = D ​ τ i → \tau=D~\vec{\tau_{i}} )
By the IH on each τ i \tau_{i} and t t -ADT .
( τ = t ​ 𝗌𝗆𝗍 \tau={t}~\mathsf{smt} )
By the IH on t t and τ \tau -SMT .
( τ = t ​ 𝗌𝗒𝗆 \tau={t}~\mathsf{sym} )
By the IH on t t and τ \tau -Sym .
( τ = 𝗆𝗈𝖽𝖾𝗅 \tau=\mathsf{model} )
Contradictory— 𝗆𝗈𝖽𝖾𝗅 \mathsf{model} is undefined for erase \mathrm{erase} .
∎
We say a type t t is an “SMT type” when Γ ⊢ 𝗌𝗆𝗍 t \Gamma\vdash_{\mathsf{smt}}t ; a
type τ \tau is an SMT type when it is equal to an SMT type t t or when
it is of the form t ​ 𝗌𝗆𝗍 {t}~\mathsf{smt} or t ​ 𝗌𝗒𝗆 {t}~\mathsf{sym} . Note that toSMT \mathrm{toSMT} always
produces an SMT type, but does not work on types that contain type
variables or the unrepresentable 𝗆𝗈𝖽𝖾𝗅 \mathsf{model} type.
Lemma D.6 (Weakening).
If ⊢ Γ \vdash\Gamma and ⊢ Γ ′ \vdash\Gamma^{\prime} and
dom ⁡ ( Γ ) ∩ dom ⁡ ( Γ ′ ) = ∅ \mathrm{dom}(\Gamma)\cap\mathrm{dom}(\Gamma^{\prime})=\emptyset then:
(1)
⊢ Γ , Γ ′ \vdash\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime}
(2)
If Γ ⊢ m τ \Gamma\vdash_{m}\tau then Γ , Γ ′ ⊢ m τ \Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash_{m}\tau ;
(3)
If Γ ⊢ e : τ \Gamma\vdash e:\tau then Γ , Γ ′ ⊢ e : τ \Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash e:\tau ; and
(4)
If Γ ⊢ ϕ : τ \Gamma\vdash\phi:\tau then Γ , Γ ′ ⊢ ϕ : τ \Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash\phi:\tau .
Proof.
By mutual induction on the derivations.
Contexts
( Γ \Gamma -Empty )
We have Γ ′ = ⋅ \Gamma^{\prime}=\cdot ; immediate by assumption.
( Γ \Gamma -Var )
We have Γ ′ = Γ ′′ , X : τ \Gamma^{\prime}=\Gamma^{\prime\prime},X:\tau . By
the IH on Γ ′′ \Gamma^{\prime\prime} and Γ \Gamma -Var , finding
Γ , Γ ′′ ⊢ m τ \Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime\prime}\vdash_{m}\tau by part ( 2 ) of
the IH.
( Γ \Gamma -TVar )
We have Γ ′ = Γ ′′ , α \Gamma^{\prime}=\Gamma^{\prime\prime},\alpha . By
the IH on Γ ′′ \Gamma^{\prime\prime} and Γ \Gamma -TVar .
Type well formedness
( t t -Base )
Immediate, by t t -Base .
( t t -TVar )
Since Γ \Gamma and Γ ′ \Gamma^{\prime} have disjoint
domains, we know α ∈ Γ \alpha\in\Gamma —by t t -TVar .
( t t -ADT )
By the IH on each constituent of D ​ τ i → D~\vec{\tau_{i}} , followed by t t -ADT .
( τ \tau -SMT )
By the IH on Γ ⊢ 𝗌𝗆𝗍 t \Gamma\vdash_{\mathsf{smt}}t and
then τ \tau -SMT .
( τ \tau -Sym )
By the IH on Γ ⊢ 𝗌𝗆𝗍 t \Gamma\vdash_{\mathsf{smt}}t and
then τ \tau -Sym .
( τ \tau -Model )
Immediate, by τ \tau -Model .
∎
Expressions
( e e -Var )
Since the domains are disjoint,
( Γ , Γ ′ ) ​ ( X ) = τ (\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime})(X)=\tau and we can still find e e -Var .
( e e -Const )
Immediate, by e e -Const .
( e e -Let )
By the e e -Let and the IH on e 1 e_{1} and
e 2 e_{2} , α \alpha -renaming X X appropriately.
( e e -Ctor )
By e e -Ctor and the IH, using part
( 2 ) on τ j ′ \tau_{j}^{\prime} and part ( 3 ) on
e i e_{i} .
( e e -Quote )
By the part ( 4 ) of the IH.
( e e -Rel )
By e e -Rel and the IH on each e i e_{i} .
( e e -Fun )
By e e -Fun and the the IH, using part
( 2 ) on τ j ′ \tau_{j}^{\prime} and part ( 3 ) on
e i e_{i} .
( e e -Op )
By e e -Op and the the IH, using part
( 2 ) on τ j ′ \tau_{j}^{\prime} and part ( 3 ) on
e i e_{i} .
( e e -If )
By e e -If and the IH on each of the e i e_{i} .
( e e -Match )
By e e -Match and the IH on e e and each
of the e i e_{i} , α \alpha -renaming each X k X_{k} appropriately.
Formulas
( ϕ \phi -Var )
By ϕ \phi -Var and part
( 2 ) of the IH.
( ϕ \phi -Promote )
By ϕ \phi -Promote and the IH.
( ϕ \phi -Unquote )
By ϕ \phi -Unquote and part
( 3 ) of the IH.
( ϕ \phi -Ctor )
By ϕ \phi -Ctor and the IH, using part
( 2 ) on the τ i \tau_{i} and part ( 4 ) on
ϕ i \phi_{i} , observing that the actual types are unchanged, and so
the toSMT \mathrm{toSMT} conversions are the same.
Lemma D.7 (Type well formedness strengthening).
If Γ , X : τ , Γ ′ ⊢ m τ ′ \Gamma,X:\tau,\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash_{m}\tau^{\prime} then Γ , Γ ′ ⊢ τ ′ \Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash\tau^{\prime} .
Proof.
( t t -Base )
Immediate.
( t t -TVar )
Immediate: removing the variable binding can’t affect α \alpha .
( t t -ADT )
By the IH on each constituent of D ​ τ i → D~\vec{\tau_{i}} .
( τ \tau -SMT )
By the IH on Γ ⊢ 𝗌𝗆𝗍 t \Gamma\vdash_{\mathsf{smt}}t .
( τ \tau -Sym )
By the IH on Γ ⊢ 𝗌𝗆𝗍 t \Gamma\vdash_{\mathsf{smt}}t .
( τ \tau -Model )
Immediate.
∎
Lemma D.8 (Type variable substitution).
If ⊢ Γ , α , Γ ′ \vdash\Gamma,\alpha,\Gamma^{\prime} and Γ ⊢ m τ ′ \Gamma\vdash_{m}\tau^{\prime} , then:
(1)
⊢ Γ , Γ ′ ​ [ τ / α ] \vdash\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime}[\tau/\alpha] ;
(2)
If Γ , α , Γ ′ ⊢ m τ \Gamma,\alpha,\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash_{m}\tau then Γ , Γ ′ [ τ / α ] ⊢ m τ ′ [ τ / α ] \Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime}[\tau/\alpha]\vdash_{m}\tau^{\prime}[\tau/\alpha] ;
(3)
If Γ , α , Γ ′ ⊢ X , τ ′ ⊳ Γ ′′ \Gamma,\alpha,\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash X,\tau^{\prime}\rhd\Gamma^{\prime\prime} then
Γ , Γ ′ ​ [ τ / α ] ⊢ X , τ ′ ​ [ τ / α ] ⊳ Γ ′′ ​ [ τ / α ] \Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime}[\tau/\alpha]\vdash X,\tau^{\prime}[\tau/\alpha]\rhd\Gamma^{\prime\prime}[\tau/\alpha] ; and
(4)
If Γ , α , Γ ′ ⊢ X i → , τ i ′ → ⊳ Γ ′′ \Gamma,\alpha,\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash\vec{X_{i}},\vec{\tau^{\prime}_{i}}\rhd\Gamma^{\prime\prime} then
Γ , Γ ′ ​ [ τ / α ] ⊢ X i → , τ i ′ → ​ [ τ / α ] ⊳ Γ ′′ ​ [ τ / α ] \Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime}[\tau/\alpha]\vdash\vec{X_{i}},\vec{\tau^{\prime}_{i}}[\tau/\alpha]\rhd\Gamma^{\prime\prime}[\tau/\alpha] .
Proof.
For parts ( 1 ) and ( 2 ), by mutual
induction on the derivations. Note that if α \alpha actually occurs
in the type, we could only have found well formedness at 𝖾𝗑𝗉 \mathsf{exp} .
( Γ \Gamma -Empty )
Contradictory: ⋅ ≠ Γ , α , Γ ′ \cdot\neq\Gamma,\alpha,\Gamma^{\prime} .
( Γ \Gamma -Var )
We have Γ ′ = Γ ′′ , X : τ ′ \Gamma^{\prime}=\Gamma^{\prime\prime},X:\tau^{\prime} where Γ , Γ ′′ ⊢ 𝖾𝗑𝗉 τ ′ \Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime\prime}\vdash_{\mathsf{exp}}\tau^{\prime} . By the IH on Γ ′′ \Gamma^{\prime\prime} , we know that ⊢ Γ , Γ ′′ ​ [ τ / α ] \vdash\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime\prime}[\tau/\alpha] ; by part ( 2 ), we have Γ , Γ ′′ [ τ / α ] ⊢ 𝖾𝗑𝗉 τ ′ [ τ / α ] \Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime\prime}[\tau/\alpha]\vdash_{\mathsf{exp}}\tau^{\prime}[\tau/\alpha] ; and so we have ⊢ Γ , Γ ′ ​ [ τ / α ] \vdash\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime}[\tau/\alpha] by Γ \Gamma -Var .
( Γ \Gamma -TVar )
We have Γ ′ = Γ ′′ , β \Gamma^{\prime}=\Gamma^{\prime\prime},\beta ; by the IH on Γ ′′ \Gamma^{\prime\prime} , we have ⊢ Γ , Γ ′′ ​ [ τ / α ] \vdash\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime\prime}[\tau/\alpha] ; since β ⁡ [ τ / α ] = β \beta[\tau/\alpha]=\beta , we can apply Γ \Gamma -TVar to find ⊢ Γ , Γ ′ ​ [ τ / α ] \vdash\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime}[\tau/\alpha] as desired.
( t t -B )
Immediate by t t -B , since B ⁡ [ τ / α ] = B B[\tau/\alpha]=B .
( t t -TVar )
We have τ ′ = β \tau^{\prime}=\beta . If α = β \alpha=\beta , then we have Γ ⊢ m α [ τ / α ] \Gamma\vdash_{m}\alpha[\tau/\alpha] by assumption. If α ≠ β \alpha\neq\beta , then it must be that β ∈ Γ \beta\in\Gamma or Γ ′ \Gamma^{\prime} —either way, β \beta is unaffected by the substitution and we have β ∈ Γ , Γ ′ ​ [ τ / α ] \beta\in\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime}[\tau/\alpha] and so Γ , Γ ′ [ τ / α ] ⊢ m β \Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime}[\tau/\alpha]\vdash_{m}\beta by t t -TVar .
( t t -ADT )
By the IH on each premise, followed by t t -ADT .
( τ \tau -SMT )
By the IH on Γ , α , Γ ′ ⊢ 𝗌𝗆𝗍 t \Gamma,\alpha,\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash_{\mathsf{smt}}t and then by τ \tau -SMT .
( τ \tau -Sym )
By the IH on Γ , α , Γ ′ ⊢ 𝗌𝗆𝗍 t \Gamma,\alpha,\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash_{\mathsf{smt}}t and then by τ \tau -Sym .
( τ \tau -Model )
Immediate by τ \tau -Model .
For parts ( 3 ) and ( 4 ), by
mutual induction on the derivations.
( X ​ τ X\tau -Bind )
We have X ∉ dom ⁡ ( Γ , α , Γ ′ ) X\not\in\mathrm{dom}(\Gamma,\alpha,\Gamma^{\prime}) , so it must also be the case that X ∉ dom ⁡ ( Γ , Γ ′ ​ [ τ / α ] ) X\not\in\mathrm{dom}(\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime}[\tau/\alpha]) . We therefore find Γ , Γ ′ ​ [ τ / α ] ⊢ X , τ ′ ​ [ τ / α ] ⊳ Γ , Γ ′ ​ [ τ / α ] , τ ′ ​ [ τ / α ] \Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime}[\tau/\alpha]\vdash X,\tau^{\prime}[\tau/\alpha]\rhd\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime}[\tau/\alpha],\tau^{\prime}[\tau/\alpha] by X ​ τ X\tau -Bind .
( X ​ τ X\tau -Check )
We have ( Γ , α , Γ ′ ) ​ ( X ) = τ ′ (\Gamma,\alpha,\Gamma^{\prime})(X)=\tau^{\prime} . Is X : τ ′ X:\tau^{\prime} in Γ \Gamma or Γ ′ \Gamma^{\prime} ? Either way we will find Γ , Γ ′ ​ [ τ / α ] ⊢ X , τ ′ ​ [ τ / α ] ⊳ Γ , Γ ′ ​ [ τ / α ] \Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime}[\tau/\alpha]\vdash X,\tau^{\prime}[\tau/\alpha]\rhd\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime}[\tau/\alpha] by X ​ τ X\tau -Check .
If τ ′ ∈ dom ⁡ ( Γ ) \tau^{\prime}\in\mathrm{dom}(\Gamma) , then Γ ⊢ τ ′ \Gamma\vdash\tau^{\prime} and so
τ ′ ​ [ τ / α ] = τ ′ \tau^{\prime}[\tau/\alpha]=\tau^{\prime} ( Γ , Γ ′ ​ [ τ / α ] ) ​ ( X ) = τ ′ (\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime}[\tau/\alpha])(X)=\tau^{\prime} and we have Γ , Γ ′ ​ [ τ / α ] ⊢ X , τ ′ ⊳ Γ , Γ ′ ​ [ τ / α ] \Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime}[\tau/\alpha]\vdash X,\tau^{\prime}\rhd\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime}[\tau/\alpha] .
If, on the other hand, τ ′ ∈ dom ⁡ ( Γ ′ ) \tau^{\prime}\in\mathrm{dom}(\Gamma^{\prime}) , then
( Γ , Γ ′ ​ [ τ / α ] ) ​ ( X ) = τ ′ ​ [ τ / α ] (\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime}[\tau/\alpha])(X)=\tau^{\prime}[\tau/\alpha] . We therefore
have Γ , Γ ′ ​ [ τ / α ] ⊢ X , τ ′ ​ [ τ / α ] ⊳ Γ , Γ ′ ​ [ τ / α ] \Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime}[\tau/\alpha]\vdash X,\tau^{\prime}[\tau/\alpha]\rhd\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime}[\tau/\alpha] .
( X → ​ τ → \vec{X}\vec{\tau} -All )
By part ( 3 ) of the IH on each premise.
∎
Lemma D.9 (Regularity; formulas have SMT types).
(1)
If ⊢ Γ \vdash\Gamma and Γ ⁡ ( X ) = τ \Gamma(X)=\tau then Γ ⊢ 𝖾𝗑𝗉 τ \Gamma\vdash_{\mathsf{exp}}\tau .
(2)
If ⊢ Φ \vdash\Phi then (a) if f : ∀ α j → , τ i → → τ ∈ Φ f:\forall\vec{\alpha_{j}},\vec{\tau_{i}}\rightarrow\tau\in\Phi
then α j → ⊢ 𝖾𝗑𝗉 τ \vec{\alpha_{j}}\vdash_{\mathsf{exp}}\tau , and (b) if 𝑢𝑓 : t i ′ → → t ∈ Φ \mathit{uf}:\vec{t_{i}^{\prime}}\rightarrow t\in\Phi then ⋅ ⊢ 𝗌𝗆𝗍 t \cdot\vdash_{\mathsf{smt}}t .
(3)
If Δ ; Φ ; Γ ⊢ e : τ \Delta;\Phi;\Gamma\vdash e:\tau then Γ ⊢ 𝖾𝗑𝗉 τ \Gamma\vdash_{\mathsf{exp}}\tau .
(4)
If
Δ ; Φ ; Γ ⊢ c 𝖼 𝖲𝖬𝖳 ​ ( ϕ i → ) : τ i → → τ \Delta;\Phi;\Gamma\vdash c^{\mathsf{SMT}}_{\mathsf{c}}(\vec{\phi_{i}}):\vec{\tau_{i}}\rightarrow\tau then τ i = t i ​ 𝗌𝗆𝗍 \tau_{i}={t_{i}}~\mathsf{smt} or
τ i = t i ​ 𝗌𝗒𝗆 \tau_{i}={t_{i}}~\mathsf{sym} and τ = t ​ 𝗌𝗆𝗍 \tau={t}~\mathsf{smt} or τ = t ​ 𝗌𝗒𝗆 \tau={t}~\mathsf{sym} and
Γ ⊢ 𝗌𝗆𝗍 τ i \Gamma\vdash_{\mathsf{smt}}\tau_{i} and
Γ ⊢ 𝗌𝗆𝗍 τ \Gamma\vdash_{\mathsf{smt}}\tau .
(5)
If Δ ; Φ ; Γ ⊢ ϕ : τ \Delta;\Phi;\Gamma\vdash\phi:\tau then τ = t ​ 𝗌𝗆𝗍 \tau={t}~\mathsf{smt} or τ = t ​ 𝗌𝗒𝗆 \tau={t}~\mathsf{sym} and
Γ ⊢ 𝗌𝗆𝗍 τ \Gamma\vdash_{\mathsf{smt}}\tau .
(6)
If Δ ; Φ ; Γ ⊢ e i → : τ i → \Delta;\Phi;\Gamma\vdash\vec{e_{i}}:\vec{\tau_{i}} then Γ ⊢ 𝖾𝗑𝗉 τ i \Gamma\vdash_{\mathsf{exp}}\tau_{i} .
(7)
If
Δ ; Φ ; Γ ⊢ ϕ i → : τ i → \Delta;\Phi;\Gamma\vdash\vec{\phi_{i}}:\vec{\tau_{i}} then
τ i = t i ​ 𝗌𝗆𝗍 \tau_{i}={t_{i}}~\mathsf{smt} or τ = t i ​ 𝗌𝗒𝗆 \tau={t_{i}}~\mathsf{sym} and Γ ⊢ 𝗌𝗆𝗍 t i \Gamma\vdash_{\mathsf{smt}}t_{i} (and so Γ ⊢ 𝖾𝗑𝗉 τ i \Gamma\vdash_{\mathsf{exp}}\tau_{i} ).
Proof.
By induction on the typing derivation.
Contexts
( Γ \Gamma -Empty )
Contradictory—there’s no way ⋅ \cdot
has a binding for X X .
( Γ \Gamma -Var )
Γ = Γ ′ , Y : τ \Gamma=\Gamma^{\prime},Y:\tau . If X = Y X=Y ,
then we know Γ ⊢ 𝖾𝗑𝗉 τ \Gamma\vdash_{\mathsf{exp}}\tau by assumption; otherwise, by
the IH on Γ ′ \Gamma^{\prime} .
( Γ \Gamma -TVar )
Γ = Γ ′ , α \Gamma=\Gamma^{\prime},\alpha . By the IH on
Γ \Gamma .
Program signatures
( Φ \Phi -Empty )
Contradictory—there are no function definitions in ⋅ \cdot .
( Φ \Phi -Fun )
Φ = Φ ′ , g : … \Phi=\Phi^{\prime},g:\dots . For case (a)
when f = g f=g , then by assumption. Otherwise, by the IH on Φ ′ \Phi^{\prime} .
( Φ \Phi -Rel )
Φ = Φ ′ , p ⊆ τ i → \Phi=\Phi^{\prime},p\subseteq\vec{\tau_{i}} . By the IH on Φ \Phi .
( Φ \Phi -UFun )
Φ = Φ ′ , 𝑢𝑓 ′ : t i ′ → → t \Phi=\Phi^{\prime},\mathit{uf}^{\prime}:\vec{t_{i}^{\prime}}\rightarrow t . For case (b) when 𝑢𝑓 = 𝑢𝑓 ′ \mathit{uf}=\mathit{uf}^{\prime} , then by
assumption. Otherwise, by the IH on Φ ′ \Phi^{\prime} .
Expressions
( e e -Var )
By the part ( 1 ) on ⊢ Γ \vdash\Gamma .
( e e -Const )
By assumption, we know that
Γ ⊢ 𝗌𝗆𝗍 typeof ( k ) \Gamma\vdash_{\mathsf{smt}}\mathrm{typeof}(k) ; by Lemma D.1
we can find Γ ⊢ 𝖾𝗑𝗉 typeof ( k ) \Gamma\vdash_{\mathsf{exp}}\mathrm{typeof}(k) .
( e e -Let )
By the IH on Γ , X : τ 1 ⊢ e 2 : τ 2 \Gamma,X:\tau_{1}\vdash e_{2}:\tau_{2} , using strengthening (Lemma D.7 ) to
find that if Γ , X : τ 1 ⊢ 𝖾𝗑𝗉 τ 2 \Gamma,X:\tau_{1}\vdash_{\mathsf{exp}}\tau_{2} then
Γ ⊢ 𝖾𝗑𝗉 τ 2 \Gamma\vdash_{\mathsf{exp}}\tau_{2} .
( e e -Ctor )
Since Γ ⊢ 𝖾𝗑𝗉 τ j ′ \Gamma\vdash_{\mathsf{exp}}\tau_{j}^{\prime} , we know
by t t -ADT that Γ ⊢ 𝖾𝗑𝗉 D τ j ′ → \Gamma\vdash_{\mathsf{exp}}D~\vec{\tau_{j}^{\prime}} .
( e e -Quote )
By the IH on part ( 5 ), we
know that Γ ⊢ 𝗌𝗆𝗍 τ \Gamma\vdash_{\mathsf{smt}}\tau (and, less relevantly, that
τ = t ​ 𝗌𝗆𝗍 \tau={t}~\mathsf{smt} or t ​ 𝗌𝗒𝗆 {t}~\mathsf{sym} ). We can find the same well
formedness at 𝖾𝗑𝗉 \mathsf{exp} by Lemma D.1 .
( e e -Rel )
Immediate by t t -B .
( e e -Fun )
Since f : ∀ α j → , τ i → → τ ∈ Φ f:\forall\vec{\alpha_{j}},\vec{\tau_{i}}\rightarrow\tau\in\Phi
and ⊢ Φ \vdash\Phi , we know by part ( 2 ) of the IH
know that α j → ⊢ 𝖾𝗑𝗉 τ \vec{\alpha_{j}}\vdash_{\mathsf{exp}}\tau . By weakening
(Lemma D.6 ) we can lift that well formedness
judgment to Γ \Gamma . Since each Γ ⊢ 𝖾𝗑𝗉 τ j ′ \Gamma\vdash_{\mathsf{exp}}\tau_{j}^{\prime} , we
can find that Γ ⊢ τ ⁡ [ τ j ′ / α j ] \Gamma\vdash\tau[\tau_{j}^{\prime}/\alpha_{j}] by substitution
(Lemma D.8 ).
( e e -Fun )
We have by assumption that typeof ⁡ ( ⊗ ) \mathrm{typeof}(\otimes)
yields a well-formed type, i.e., α j → ⊢ 𝖾𝗑𝗉 τ \vec{\alpha_{j}}\vdash_{\mathsf{exp}}\tau
(and also for each τ i \tau_{i} ). By weakening
(Lemma D.6 ) we can lift that well formedness
judgment to Γ \Gamma . Since each Γ ⊢ 𝖾𝗑𝗉 τ j ′ \Gamma\vdash_{\mathsf{exp}}\tau_{j}^{\prime} , we
can find that Γ ⊢ τ ⁡ [ τ j ′ / α j ] \Gamma\vdash\tau[\tau_{j}^{\prime}/\alpha_{j}] by substitution
(Lemma D.8 ).
( e e -If )
By the IH on Γ ⊢ e 2 : t \Gamma\vdash e_{2}:t .
( e e -Match )
By the IH on
Γ , X 1 : τ 1 ​ [ τ j / α j ] → ⊢ e 1 : τ \Gamma,\overrightarrow{X_{1}:\tau_{1}[\tau_{j}/\alpha_{j}]}\vdash e_{1}:\tau we have
Γ , X 1 : τ 1 ​ [ τ j / α j ] → ⊢ τ \Gamma,\overrightarrow{X_{1}:\tau_{1}[\tau_{j}/\alpha_{j}]}\vdash\tau ;
we can use strengthening (Lemma D.7 ) to find
Γ ⊢ 𝖾𝗑𝗉 τ \Gamma\vdash_{\mathsf{exp}}\tau .
SMT constructors
( c c -SMT-Var )
Immediate, with Γ ⊢ 𝗌𝗆𝗍 t \Gamma\vdash_{\mathsf{smt}}t
coming from the rule itself.
( c c -SMT-Const )
Immediate, since we have by assumption
that ⋅ ⊢ 𝗌𝗆𝗍 typeof ( k ) \cdot\vdash_{\mathsf{smt}}\mathrm{typeof}(k) .
( c c -SMT-Let )
Immediate, with the necessary well
formedness assumptions coming from the rule itself.
( c c -SMT-Ctor )
We know that D ​ t j ′ → D~\vec{t_{j}^{\prime}} is well
formed by t t -ADT . We can find the translation of the
argument types well formed by Lemma D.4 on each of
the Γ ⊢ 𝗌𝗆𝗍 τ i [ t j ′ / α j ] \Gamma\vdash_{\mathsf{smt}}\tau_{i}[t_{j}^{\prime}/\alpha_{j}] derivations.
( c c -SMT-Forall )
Immediate, with the necessary
Γ ⊢ 𝗌𝗆𝗍 t 1 \Gamma\vdash_{\mathsf{smt}}t_{1} coming from the rule itself.
( c c -SMT-UFun )
Since ⊢ Φ \vdash\Phi and 𝑢𝑓 : t i → → t ∈ Φ \mathit{uf}:\vec{t_{i}}\rightarrow t\in\Phi , we know that
⋅ ⊢ 𝗌𝗆𝗍 t \cdot\vdash_{\mathsf{smt}}t by part ( 2 ) of the IH and
so ⋅ ⊢ 𝗌𝗆𝗍 t 𝗌𝗆𝗍 \cdot\vdash_{\mathsf{smt}}{t}~\mathsf{smt} , which we can lift to Γ \Gamma by
weakening (Lemma D.6 ).
Formulas
( ϕ \phi -Promote )
Since Δ ; Φ ; Γ ⊢ ϕ : t ​ 𝗌𝗒𝗆 \Delta;\Phi;\Gamma\vdash\phi:{t}~\mathsf{sym} , we know that Γ ⊢ 𝗌𝗆𝗍 t \Gamma\vdash_{\mathsf{smt}}t and so we are
correct in yielding τ = t ​ 𝗌𝗆𝗍 \tau={t}~\mathsf{smt} .
( ϕ \phi -Unquote )
We have Δ ; Φ ; Γ ⊢ e : τ \Delta;\Phi;\Gamma\vdash e:\tau such that Γ ⊢ 𝗌𝗆𝗍 τ \Gamma\vdash_{\mathsf{smt}}\tau . By
Lemma D.4 we know that toSMT ⁡ ( τ ) \mathrm{toSMT}(\tau) is a well
formed SMT type.
( ϕ \phi -Ctor )
By the IH part ( 4 ) on Γ ⊢ c 𝖼 𝖲𝖬𝖳 : τ i → → τ \Gamma\vdash c^{\mathsf{SMT}}_{\mathsf{c}}:\vec{\tau_{i}}\rightarrow\tau .
Vectored expressions and formulas
By the IH for parts ( 3 ) and ( 5 ),
respectively.
∎
Lemma D.10 (SMT value conversion is type correct).
If Δ ; Φ ; Γ ⊢ v : τ \Delta;\Phi;\Gamma\vdash v:\tau and Γ ⊢ 𝗌𝗆𝗍 τ \Gamma\vdash_{\mathsf{smt}}\tau then
Δ ; Φ ; Γ ⊢ toSMT ⁡ ( v ) : toSMT ⁡ ( τ ) \Delta;\Phi;\Gamma\vdash\mathrm{toSMT}(v):\mathrm{toSMT}(\tau) .
Proof.
First, observe that τ \tau and all of its parts must be well formed
at 𝗌𝗆𝗍 \mathsf{smt} , by Lemmas D.5
and D.3 .
By induction on the typing derivation. In expression mode, the
applicable rules are e e -Const and e e -Ctor ; only a few
typing rules could even have applied to a value in formula mode:
the ϕ \phi -Ctor and ϕ \phi -Promote .
( e e -Const )
We have Γ ⊢ k : typeof ⁡ ( k ) \Gamma\vdash k:\mathrm{typeof}(k) ;
since toSMT ⁡ ( k ) = c 𝖼𝗈𝗇𝗌𝗍 𝖲𝖬𝖳 ​ [ k ] ​ ( ) \mathrm{toSMT}(k)=c^{\mathsf{SMT}}_{\mathsf{const}}[k]() and toSMT ⁡ ( typeof ⁡ ( k ) ) = k ​ 𝗌𝗆𝗍 \mathrm{toSMT}(\mathrm{typeof}(k))={k}~\mathsf{smt}
(since Γ ⊢ 𝗌𝗆𝗍 typeof ( k ) \Gamma\vdash_{\mathsf{smt}}\mathrm{typeof}(k) by assumption), we must show
that Γ ⊢ c 𝖼𝗈𝗇𝗌𝗍 𝖲𝖬𝖳 ​ [ k ] ​ ( ) : typeof ⁡ ( k ) ​ 𝗌𝗆𝗍 \Gamma\vdash c^{\mathsf{SMT}}_{\mathsf{const}}[k]():{\mathrm{typeof}(k)}~\mathsf{smt} , which we have
by ϕ \phi -SMT-Const .
( e e -Ctor )
We have v = c ⁡ ( v i → ) v=c(\vec{v_{i}}) and:
Δ ( D ) = ∀ α j → , { … , c : τ i → , … } Γ ⊢ 𝖾𝗑𝗉 τ j ′ Γ ⊢ v i : τ i [ τ j ′ / α j ] \Delta(D)=\forall\vec{\alpha_{j}},\{\dots,c:\vec{\tau_{i}},\dots\}\quad\quad\Gamma\vdash_{\mathsf{exp}}\tau_{j}^{\prime}\quad\quad\Gamma\vdash v_{i}:\tau_{i}[\tau_{j}^{\prime}/\alpha_{j}]
Further, we know that Γ ⊢ 𝗌𝗆𝗍 D τ j ′ → \Gamma\vdash_{\mathsf{smt}}D~\vec{\tau_{j}^{\prime}} (and
so each of the subderivations must also be 𝗌𝗆𝗍 \mathsf{smt} ) and that
toSMT ⁡ ( c ⁡ ( v i → ) ) = c 𝖼𝗍𝗈𝗋 𝖲𝖬𝖳 ​ [ c ] ​ ( toSMT ⁡ ( v i ) → ) \mathrm{toSMT}(c(\vec{v_{i}}))=c^{\mathsf{SMT}}_{\mathsf{ctor}}[c](\overrightarrow{\mathrm{toSMT}(v_{i})}) .
By the IH on each of these v i v_{i} , we know that we find appropriate
values at appropriately converted types, i.e.,
Γ ⊢ toSMT ⁡ ( v i ) : toSMT ⁡ ( τ i ​ [ τ j ′ / α j ] ) \Gamma\vdash\mathrm{toSMT}(v_{i}):\mathrm{toSMT}(\tau_{i}[\tau_{j}^{\prime}/\alpha_{j}]) .
By Lemma D.4 , we know
toSMT ⁡ ( τ i ​ [ τ j ′ / α j ] ) \mathrm{toSMT}(\tau_{i}[\tau_{j}^{\prime}/\alpha_{j}]) is some well formed SMT type. We
are almost able to apply ϕ \phi -SMT-Ctor , but we must
pick appropriate t j ′ t_{j}^{\prime} . We know that toSMT ⁡ ( τ j ′ ) \mathrm{toSMT}(\tau_{j}^{\prime}) is a well
formed SMT type of the form t j ′ ​ 𝗌𝗆𝗍 {t_{j}^{\prime}}~\mathsf{smt} or t j ′ ​ 𝗌𝗒𝗆 {t_{j}^{\prime}}~\mathsf{sym}
(Lemma D.4 ). Whether it’s symbolic or not, let
the inner t j ′ t_{j}^{\prime} there be our t j ′ t_{j}^{\prime} . We can now apply ϕ \phi -SMT-Ctor to
find that Γ ⊢ toSMT ⁡ ( c ⁡ ( v i → ) ) : toSMT ⁡ ( D ​ τ j ′ → ) \Gamma\vdash\mathrm{toSMT}(c(\vec{v_{i}})):\mathrm{toSMT}(D~\vec{\tau_{j}^{\prime}}) .
( ϕ \phi -Promote )
By the IH on Γ ⊢ v : t ​ 𝗌𝗒𝗆 \Gamma\vdash v:{t}~\mathsf{sym} , we know that Γ ⊢ toSMT ⁡ ( v ) : toSMT ⁡ ( t ​ 𝗌𝗒𝗆 ) \Gamma\vdash\mathrm{toSMT}(v):\mathrm{toSMT}({t}~\mathsf{sym}) , i.e., Γ ⊢ toSMT ⁡ ( v ) : t ​ 𝗌𝗒𝗆 \Gamma\vdash\mathrm{toSMT}(v):{t}~\mathsf{sym} . By
reapplying ϕ \phi -Promote we can find that
Γ ⊢ toSMT ⁡ ( v ) : v ​ 𝗌𝗆𝗍 \Gamma\vdash\mathrm{toSMT}(v):{v}~\mathsf{smt} .
( ϕ \phi -Ctor )
Immediate: toSMT \mathrm{toSMT} does nothing to the
c … 𝖲𝖬𝖳 c^{\mathsf{SMT}}_{\mathsf{\dots}} constructed value nor to the SMT-type τ \tau assigned
to it (which is t ​ 𝗌𝗆𝗍 {t}~\mathsf{smt} in all cases except for c 𝗏𝖺𝗋 𝖲𝖬𝖳 c^{\mathsf{SMT}}_{\mathsf{var}} ).
∎
Appendix E Type safety
To prove type safety, we prove two properties for every mode of evaluation:
first, it is safe , i.e, never yields ⊥ \bot ;
and second, it is type preserving , i.e., well typed inputs yield
well typed outputs.
The proofs are fairly conventional. For all but the last step, we
prove safety and type preservation simultaneously.
We start with expressions and formulas
(Lemma E.2 ), which requires a modest
notion of canonical forms (Lemma E.1 ).
Next, we prove that value unification
(Lemma E.3 ) is type preserving,
reasoning about unification in general within the lemma showing safety
and type preservation for premises (Lemma E.4 ).
After a brief lemma about bindings (Lemma E.5 ), we
can prove that program evaluation is safe
(Theorem E.6 ) and type preserving
(Theorem E.7 ).
Lemma E.1 (Canonical forms for t ​ 𝗌𝗒𝗆 {t}~\mathsf{sym} ).
If Δ ; Φ ; Γ ⊢ v : t ​ 𝗌𝗒𝗆 \Delta;\Phi;\Gamma\vdash v:{t}~\mathsf{sym} then v = c 𝗏𝖺𝗋 𝖲𝖬𝖳 ​ [ x , t ] ​ ( ) v=c^{\mathsf{SMT}}_{\mathsf{var}}[x,t]() .
Proof.
The only typing rule that could have applied is ϕ \phi -SMT-Var .
∎
Lemma E.2 (Term and formula type safety).
If Δ ; Φ ⊧ 𝒲 \Delta;\Phi\models\mathcal{W} and Δ ; Φ ⊢ F → \Delta;\Phi\vdash\vec{F}
and Γ ⊧ θ \Gamma\models\theta , when either:
(1)
Δ ; Φ ; Γ ⊢ e : τ \Delta;\Phi;\Gamma\vdash e:\tau and
𝒲 ; θ ⊢ e ⇓ e v ⊥ \mathcal{W};\theta\vdash e\Downarrow_{e}v_{\bot} ; or
(2)
Δ ; Φ ; Γ ⊢ ϕ : τ \Delta;\Phi;\Gamma\vdash\phi:\tau and 𝒲 ; θ ⊢ ϕ ⇓ ϕ v ⊥ \mathcal{W};\theta\vdash\phi\Downarrow_{\phi}v_{\bot}
(3)
Δ ; Φ ⊢ 𝖿𝗎𝗇 ​ f ​ ( X i → : τ i → ) : τ = e \Delta;\Phi\vdash\mathsf{fun}~{f}({\vec{X_{i}}:\vec{\tau_{i}}}):{\tau}={e} and α j → , X i : τ i → ⊧ θ ′ \vec{\alpha_{j}},\overrightarrow{X_{i}:\tau_{i}}\models\theta^{\prime} and 𝒲 ; θ ′ ⊢ e ⇓ e v ⊥ \mathcal{W};\theta^{\prime}\vdash e\Downarrow_{e}v_{\bot}
then v ⊥ = v v_{\bot}=v (i.e., v ⊥ ≠ ⊥ v_{\bot}\neq\bot ) and
Δ ; Φ ; Γ ⊢ v : τ \Delta;\Phi;\Gamma\vdash v:\tau .
Similarly, when either:
(1)
if Δ ; Φ ; Γ ⊢ e i : τ i \Delta;\Phi;\Gamma\vdash e_{i}:\tau_{i} and 𝒲 ; θ ⊢ e i → ⇓ e v i → \mathcal{W};\theta\vdash\vec{e_{i}}\Downarrow_{e}\vec{v_{i}}
then Δ ; Φ ; Γ ⊢ v i → ⊥ : τ i → \Delta;\Phi;\Gamma\vdash\vec{v_{i}}_{\bot}:\vec{\tau_{i}} ; and
(2)
if
Δ ; Φ ; Γ ⊢ ϕ i : τ i \Delta;\Phi;\Gamma\vdash\phi_{i}:\tau_{i} and
𝒲 ; θ ⊢ ϕ i → ⇓ ϕ → v i → \mathcal{W};\theta\vdash\vec{\phi_{i}}\Downarrow_{\vec{\phi}}\vec{v_{i}} then
Δ ; Φ ; Γ ⊢ v i → ⊥ : τ \Delta;\Phi;\Gamma\vdash\vec{v_{i}}_{\bot}:\tau
then v i → ⊥ = v i → \vec{v_{i}}_{\bot}=\vec{v_{i}} (i.e., it is not ⊥ \bot ) and
Δ ; Φ ; Γ ⊢ v i : τ i \Delta;\Phi;\Gamma\vdash v_{i}:\tau_{i} .
Proof.
By mutual induction on derivations and the length of the vectored
expressions/formulas, leaving θ \theta general (for,
e.g., e e -Let and e e -Match ).
Expressions
( e e -Var )
We have Γ ⁡ ( X ) = τ \Gamma(X)=\tau ; since
Γ ⊧ θ \Gamma\models\theta , we have θ ⁡ ( X ) = v \theta(X)=v (and
so ⇓ e \Downarrow_{e} -Var-E didn’t apply). So it must be the case
that ⇓ e \Downarrow_{e} -Var applied. We can see further that
Δ ; Φ ; ⋅ ⊢ v : τ \Delta;\Phi;\cdot\vdash v:\tau , and we are done by weakening
(Lemma D.6 ).
( e e -Const )
It must be that ⇓ e \Downarrow_{e} -Const
applied, and we immediately see that v ⊥ ≠ ⊥ v_{\bot}\neq\bot and k k is
well typed in any well formed context by assumption
and e e -Const .
( e e -Let )
We know that Γ ⊢ e 1 : τ 1 \Gamma\vdash e_{1}:\tau_{1} and
Γ , X : τ 1 ⊢ e 2 : τ 2 \Gamma,X:\tau_{1}\vdash e_{2}:\tau_{2} . By the IH on e 1 e_{1} , we know
that θ ; 𝒲 ⊢ e 1 ⇓ e v 1 \theta;\mathcal{W}\vdash e_{1}\Downarrow_{e}v_{1} , so it can’t be the
case that ⇓ e \Downarrow_{e} -Let-E applied—it must hae
been ⇓ e \Downarrow_{e} -Let . By the IH on e 2 e_{2} , we know that the
final result is also not ⊥ \bot and is well typed.
( e e -Ctor )
We have Δ ( D ) = ∀ α j → , { … , c : τ i → , … } \Delta(D)=\forall\vec{\alpha_{j}},\{\dots,c:\vec{\tau_{i}},\dots\} and
Γ ⊢ e i : τ i ​ [ τ j ′ / α j ] \Gamma\vdash e_{i}:\tau_{i}[\tau_{j}^{\prime}/\alpha_{j}] . By the IH, we know
that each of the e i → \vec{e_{i}} must have reduced to non- ⊥ \bot values,
and so ⇓ e \Downarrow_{e} -Ctor-E could not have applied. We can
therefore see that each e i e_{i} reduces to an appropriately typed
v i v_{i} , and our resulting value is well typed by e e -Ctor .
( e e -Quote )
Only ⇓ e \Downarrow_{e} -Quote could have
applied. By the IH, we know that ϕ \phi reduces to a non- ⊥ \bot
value v v well typed at τ \tau .
( e e -Rel )
We know that
p ⊆ τ i → ∈ Φ p\subseteq\vec{\tau_{i}}\in\Phi and Γ ⊢ e i : τ i \Gamma\vdash e_{i}:\tau_{i} . The IH on e i → \vec{e_{i}} rules out S ​ t ​ e ​ p ​ s ​ t ​ o ​ E StepstoE -Rel-E1 ;
the typing rule rules out the arity mismatch
in ⇓ e \Downarrow_{e} -Rel-E2 and the missing relation
in ⇓ e \Downarrow_{e} -Rel-E3 . So it must be the case
that ⇓ e \Downarrow_{e} -Rel-True or ⇓ e \Downarrow_{e} -Rel-False
applied; either way, we yield a 𝖻𝗈𝗈𝗅 \mathsf{bool} , which is appropriately
typed by e e -Const .
( e e -Fun )
We know that f : ∀ α j → , τ i → → τ ∈ Φ f:\forall\vec{\alpha_{j}},\vec{\tau_{i}}\rightarrow\tau\in\Phi
and Γ ⊢ e i : τ i ​ [ τ j ′ / α j ] \Gamma\vdash e_{i}:\tau_{i}[\tau_{j}^{\prime}/\alpha_{j}] . The IH on
e i → \vec{e_{i}} rules out S ​ t ​ e ​ p ​ s ​ t ​ o ​ E StepstoE -Fun-E1 ; the typing rule rules
out the arity mismatch in ⇓ e \Downarrow_{e} -Fun-E2 and the missing
function in ⇓ e \Downarrow_{e} -Fun-E3 . So it must be the case
that ⇓ e \Downarrow_{e} -Fun applied. Since Δ ; Φ ⊢ F \Delta;\Phi\vdash F , we
know by the IH on part ( 3 ) that the resulting value
is non- ⊥ \bot and well typed at τ ⁡ [ τ j ′ / α j ] \tau[\tau_{j}^{\prime}/\alpha_{j}] .
( e e -Op )
We know that typeof ⁡ ( ⊗ ) = ∀ α j → , τ i → → τ \mathrm{typeof}(\otimes)=\forall\vec{\alpha_{j}},\vec{\tau_{i}}\rightarrow\tau and
Γ ⊢ e i : τ i ​ [ τ j ′ / α j ] \Gamma\vdash e_{i}:\tau_{i}[\tau_{j}^{\prime}/\alpha_{j}] . The IH on
e i → \vec{e_{i}} rules out S ​ t ​ e ​ p ​ s ​ t ​ o ​ E StepstoE -Op-E1 ; the typing rule rules
out the arity/domain mismatch in ⇓ e \Downarrow_{e} -Op-E2 . So it must
be the case that ⇓ e \Downarrow_{e} -Op applied. We know that the
result is well typed by our assumption that typeof ⁡ ( ⊗ ) \mathrm{typeof}(\otimes) and
[ [ ⊗ ] ] [\![\otimes]\!] agree.
( e e -If )
We have Γ ⊢ e 1 : 𝖻𝗈𝗈𝗅 \Gamma\vdash e_{1}:\mathsf{bool} and
Γ ⊢ e 2 : τ \Gamma\vdash e_{2}:\tau and Γ ⊢ e 3 : τ \Gamma\vdash e_{3}:\tau .
By the IH on e 1 e_{1} , we know that e 1 e_{1} reduces to true \mathrm{true} or
false \mathrm{false} (since those are the only values of type 𝖻𝗈𝗈𝗅 \mathsf{bool} ). So we
can rule out ⇓ e \Downarrow_{e} -Ite-E1
and ⇓ e \Downarrow_{e} -Ite-E2 —we must have stepped by
either ⇓ e \Downarrow_{e} -Ite-T or ⇓ e \Downarrow_{e} -Ite-F .
The IH on e 2 e_{2} or e 3 e_{3} (respectively) guarantees we step to a
non- ⊥ \bot , well typed value.
( e e -Match )
We have Γ ⊢ e : D ​ τ j → \Gamma\vdash e:D~\vec{\tau_{j}}
and Δ ( D ) = ∀ α j → , { … , c i : τ k → , … } \Delta(D)=\forall\vec{\alpha_{j}},\{\dots,c_{i}:\vec{\tau_{k}},\dots\} and
Γ , X k : τ k ​ [ τ j / α j ] → ⊢ e i : τ \Gamma,\overrightarrow{X_{k}:\tau_{k}[\tau_{j}/\alpha_{j}]}\vdash e_{i}:\tau .
The IH on e e guarantees that we get a non- ⊥ \bot value at type D ​ τ j → D~\vec{\tau_{j}} , which rules out the error
case ⇓ e \Downarrow_{e} -Match-E1 , the non-constructor value
of ⇓ e \Downarrow_{e} -Match-E2 , the mis-named constructor
of ⇓ e \Downarrow_{e} -Match-E3 , and the arity error
of ⇓ e \Downarrow_{e} -Match-E4 .
So it must be the case that we applied ⇓ e \Downarrow_{e} -Match ; by
the IH, the matching pattern reduces to a well typed non- ⊥ \bot
value.
Formulas
( ϕ \phi -Promote )
We have
Δ ; Φ ; Γ ⊢ ϕ : t ​ 𝗌𝗒𝗆 \Delta;\Phi;\Gamma\vdash\phi:{t}~\mathsf{sym} ; by the IH, we know
that ϕ \phi steps to a non- ⊥ \bot value v v well typed at t ​ 𝗌𝗒𝗆 {t}~\mathsf{sym} ;
by ϕ \phi -Promote we can see that v v is also well typed at
t ​ 𝗌𝗆𝗍 {t}~\mathsf{smt} .
( ϕ \phi -Unquote )
We have , ​ e \textbf{,}{e} ; since
Δ ; Φ ; Γ ⊢ e : τ \Delta;\Phi;\Gamma\vdash e:\tau , we know by the IH that e e
reduces to a non- ⊥ \bot value v v that is also well typed at τ \tau .
We can therefore rule out ⇓ ϕ \Downarrow_{\phi} -Unquote-E , so we must
have stepped by ⇓ ϕ \Downarrow_{\phi} -Unquote .
Since Δ ; Φ ; Γ ⊢ v : τ \Delta;\Phi;\Gamma\vdash v:\tau and Γ ⊢ 𝗌𝗆𝗍 τ \Gamma\vdash_{\mathsf{smt}}\tau , we have
Δ ; Φ ; Γ ⊢ toSMT ⁡ ( v ) : toSMT ⁡ ( τ ) \Delta;\Phi;\Gamma\vdash\mathrm{toSMT}(v):\mathrm{toSMT}(\tau) by
Lemma D.10 , as desired.
( ϕ \phi -Ctor )
We have c 𝖼 𝖲𝖬𝖳 ​ ( ϕ i → ) c^{\mathsf{SMT}}_{\mathsf{c}}(\vec{\phi_{i}}) such
that Γ ⊢ c 𝖼 𝖲𝖬𝖳 : τ i → → τ \Gamma\vdash c^{\mathsf{SMT}}_{\mathsf{c}}:\vec{\tau_{i}}\rightarrow\tau and
Γ ⊢ ϕ i : τ i \Gamma\vdash\phi_{i}:\tau_{i} . We know by the IH that each
ϕ i \phi_{i} is well typed at τ i \tau_{i} and so none of them step to
⊥ \bot , and so ⇓ ϕ \Downarrow_{\phi} -Ctor-E cannot apply.
Therefore either ⇓ ϕ \Downarrow_{\phi} -Ctor or ⇓ ϕ \Downarrow_{\phi} -Value
applied; the resulting value is well typed by the IH or remains well
typed, respectively.

By part ( 1 ) on
α j → , X i : τ i → ⊢ e : τ \vec{\alpha_{j}},\overrightarrow{X_{i}:\tau_{i}}\vdash e:\tau , using

Lemma E.3 (Value unification preservation).
If Γ ⊧ θ \Gamma\models\theta when either:
(1)
Γ ⊢ X → , τ → ⊳ Γ ′ \Gamma\vdash\vec{X},\vec{\tau}\rhd\Gamma^{\prime} and
Γ ⊢ v → : τ → \Gamma\vdash\vec{v}:\vec{\tau} and
θ ⊢ X → ∼ v → : θ ′ \theta\vdash\vec{X}\sim\vec{v}:\theta^{\prime} ; or
(2)
Γ ⊢ X , τ ⊳ Γ ′ \Gamma\vdash X,\tau\rhd\Gamma^{\prime} and Γ ⊢ v : τ \Gamma\vdash v:\tau and θ ⊢ X ∼ v ⊳ θ ′ \theta\vdash X\sim v\rhd\theta^{\prime} ;
then Γ ′ ⊧ θ ′ \Gamma^{\prime}\models\theta^{\prime} .
Proof.
By induction on the derivation of well typing.
( X ​ τ X\tau -Bind )
Only u ​ v uv -Bind-Var could have
applied, so we have Γ ⊢ v : τ \Gamma\vdash v:\tau and
Γ ⊧ θ \Gamma\models\theta and must show that
Γ , X : τ ⊧ θ [ X ↦ v ] \Gamma,X:\tau\models\theta[X\mapsto v] , which we have
immediately.
( X ​ τ X\tau -Check )
Here X ∈ Γ X\in\Gamma , so it must be that
θ ⁡ ( X ) \theta(X) is defined. One of three rules could have applied:
( u ​ v uv -Eq-Var )
We have Γ ′ = Γ \Gamma^{\prime}=\Gamma and θ ′ = θ \theta^{\prime}=\theta , so Γ ′ ⊧ θ ′ \Gamma^{\prime}\models\theta^{\prime} by assumption.
( u ​ v uv -Ctor )
By the IH, we know that Γ ′ ​ m ​ o ​ d ​ e ​ l ​ s ​ θ ′ \Gamma^{\prime}models\theta^{\prime} .
( u ​ v uv -Constant )
As for u ​ v uv -Eq-Var , we have
Γ ′ = Γ \Gamma^{\prime}=\Gamma and θ ′ = θ \theta^{\prime}=\theta , so
Γ ′ ⊧ θ ′ \Gamma^{\prime}\models\theta^{\prime} by assumption.
( X ​ τ X\tau -All )
It must be that u → ​ v → \vec{u}\vec{v} -All
applied; by the IH on each sub-derivation, we can find that
Γ i ⊧ θ i \Gamma_{i}\models\theta_{i} , and so Γ ′ ⊧ θ ′ \Gamma^{\prime}\models\theta^{\prime} in
particular.
∎
User code will never directly trigger a use
of u ​ v uv -Eq-Var directly, because the unification rules won’t call
value unification with a defined LHS (we’d just use u ​ u uu -BB
instead). But a use of u → ​ v → \vec{u}\vec{v} -All could lead to a
variable being unified early on and then used again in the same
unification process.
Lemma E.4 (Premise preservation and safety).
If Δ ; Φ ; Γ ⊢ P ⊳ Γ ′ \Delta;\Phi;\Gamma\vdash P\rhd\Gamma^{\prime} and
Δ ; Φ ⊧ F → \Delta;\Phi\models\vec{F} and Δ ; Φ ⊧ 𝒲 \Delta;\Phi\models\mathcal{W} and
Γ ⊧ θ \Gamma\models\theta then
if F → ; 𝒲 ; θ ⊢ P → θ ⊥ ′ \vec{F};\mathcal{W};\theta\vdash P\rightarrow\theta^{\prime}_{\bot} then:
(1)
θ ⊥ ′ = θ ′ \theta^{\prime}_{\bot}=\theta^{\prime} (i.e.,
it is not ⊥ \bot ); and
(2)
Γ ′ ⊧ θ ′ \Gamma^{\prime}\models\theta^{\prime} .
Proof.
By induction on the premise typing derivation, followed by cases on the step taken.
( P P -PosAtom )
We have:
p ⊆ τ i → ∈ Φ Γ ⊢ X i → , τ i → ⊳ Γ ′ p\subseteq\vec{\tau_{i}}\in\Phi\quad\quad\Gamma\vdash\vec{X_{i}},\vec{\tau_{i}}\rhd\Gamma^{\prime}
The only rule that could have applied is PosAtom , i.e.,
v → ∈ 𝒲 ⁡ ( p ) \vec{v}\in\mathcal{W}(p) and
θ ⊢ X i → ∼ v i → : θ ⊥ ′ \theta\vdash\vec{X_{i}}\sim\vec{v_{i}}:\theta^{\prime}_{\bot} . We must
show that θ ⊥ ′ = θ ′ \theta^{\prime}_{\bot}=\theta^{\prime} and Γ ′ ⊧ θ ′ \Gamma^{\prime}\models\theta^{\prime} .
Since Δ ; Φ ⊧ 𝒲 \Delta;\Phi\models\mathcal{W} , we know that
⋅ ⊢ v i → : τ i → \cdot\vdash\vec{v_{i}}:\vec{\tau_{i}} ; by weakening we have
Γ ⊢ v i → : τ i → \Gamma\vdash\vec{v_{i}}:\vec{\tau_{i}}
(Lemma D.6 ).
Syntactically, we know that X i → \vec{X_{i}} are all variables and that
v i → \vec{v_{i}} are all values. For each one, therefore only two
unification rules could possibly apply: u ​ u uu -BB ( X i X_{i} is
bound) and u ​ u uu -FB ( X i X_{i} is free). In
particular, u ​ u uu -FF cannot apply, and so we cannot
produce ⊥ \bot , so θ ⊥ ′ = θ ′ = θ ​ θ i → \theta^{\prime}_{\bot}=\theta^{\prime}=\theta\vec{\theta_{i}} .
By Lemma E.3 , we know that
Γ ′ ⊧ θ ​ θ i \Gamma^{\prime}\models\theta\theta_{i} for each i i , and so
Γ ′ ⊧ θ ​ θ i → \Gamma^{\prime}\models\theta\vec{\theta_{i}} .
( P P -NegAtom )
We have:
p ⊆ τ i → ∈ Φ Γ ⊢ X i → , τ i → ⊳ Γ \begin{array}[]{c}p\subseteq\vec{\tau_{i}}\in\Phi\quad\quad\Gamma\vdash\vec{X_{i}},\vec{\tau_{i}}\rhd\Gamma\end{array}
Two rules are possible: NegAtom and NegAtom-E . We must
show that the latter cannot apply and that the former preserves
typing.
Since Γ ⊧ θ \Gamma\models\theta , it must be that case that each
X i → ∈ dom ⁡ ( θ ) \vec{X_{i}}\in\mathrm{dom}(\theta) , and so NegAtom-E cannot have
applied.
It remains to be seen that Γ ⊧ θ ′ \Gamma\models\theta^{\prime} —but
in NegAtom we have θ = θ ′ \theta=\theta^{\prime} , and so we are done.
( P P -EqCtor-BF )
We have:
Δ ( D ) = ∀ α j → , { … , c : τ i → , … } Γ ⊢ Y , τ ⁡ [ τ j ′ → / α j → ] ⊳ Γ X i → ⊈ Γ Γ ⊢ X i → , τ i → ​ [ τ j ′ → / α j → ] ⊳ Γ ′ \begin{array}[]{c}\Delta(D)=\forall\vec{\alpha_{j}},\{\dots,c:\vec{\tau_{i}},\dots\}\\
\Gamma\vdash Y,\tau[\vec{\tau_{j}^{\prime}}/\vec{\alpha_{j}}]\rhd\Gamma\quad\quad\vec{X_{i}}\nsubseteq\Gamma\quad\quad\Gamma\vdash\vec{X_{i}},\vec{\tau_{i}}[\vec{\tau_{j}^{\prime}}/\vec{\alpha_{j}}]\rhd\Gamma^{\prime}\end{array}
The only rule that could have applied is EqCtor , where
θ ⊢ Y ​ c ​ ( X i → ) : θ ⊥ ′ \theta\vdash Y~c(\vec{X_{i}}):\theta^{\prime}_{\bot} . We must show that
θ ⊥ ′ = θ ′ \theta^{\prime}_{\bot}=\theta^{\prime} (i.e., it is not ⊥ \bot ) and that
Γ ′ ⊧ θ ′ \Gamma^{\prime}\models\theta^{\prime} .
Since Γ ⊢ Y , τ ⁡ [ τ j ′ → / α j → ] ⊳ Γ \Gamma\vdash Y,\tau[\vec{\tau_{j}^{\prime}}/\vec{\alpha_{j}}]\rhd\Gamma , it must be the
case that Y ∈ dom ⁡ ( Γ ) Y\in\mathrm{dom}(\Gamma) and so θ ⁡ ( Y ) = v \theta(Y)=v (and so
⋅ ⊢ v : τ ⁡ [ τ j ′ → / α j → ] \cdot\vdash v:\tau[\vec{\tau_{j}^{\prime}}/\vec{\alpha_{j}}] , which also
holds under Γ \Gamma thanks to weakening
(Lemma D.6 )).
Only two rules could have applied to show θ ⊢ Y ​ c ​ ( X i → ) : θ ⊥ ′ \theta\vdash Y~c(\vec{X_{i}}):\theta^{\prime}_{\bot} : u ​ u uu -BF (when some of X i → \vec{X_{i}}
are unbound) or u ​ u uu -BB (when all of the X i → \vec{X_{i}} are
bound). In either case, u ​ u uu -FF can’t have a applied, and so
θ ⊥ ′ = θ ′ \theta^{\prime}_{\bot}=\theta^{\prime} .
One of two rules could have applied: u ​ v uv -Eq-Var
or u ​ v uv -Ctor .
In the former case, we applied u ​ u uu -BB , because
θ ⁡ ( c ⁡ ( X i → ) ) = c ⁡ ( v i → ) \theta(c(\vec{X_{i}}))=c(\vec{v_{i}}) . We have θ ′ = θ [ X ↦ c ( v i → ) \theta^{\prime}=\theta[X\mapsto c(\vec{v_{i}}) and Γ ′ ⊧ θ ′ \Gamma^{\prime}\models\theta^{\prime} by
substitution on
Γ ⊢ X i → , τ i → ​ [ τ j ′ → / α j → ] ⊳ Γ ′ \Gamma\vdash\vec{X_{i}},\vec{\tau_{i}}[\vec{\tau_{j}^{\prime}}/\vec{\alpha_{j}}]\rhd\Gamma^{\prime}
(Lemma D.8 ).
In the latter case, we can find that Γ ′ ⊧ θ ′ \Gamma^{\prime}\models\theta^{\prime} by
Lemma E.3 on the assumption that
Γ ⊢ X i → , τ i → ​ [ τ j ′ → / α j → ] ⊳ Γ ′ \Gamma\vdash\vec{X_{i}},\vec{\tau_{i}}[\vec{\tau_{j}^{\prime}}/\vec{\alpha_{j}}]\rhd\Gamma^{\prime} ,
and the fact θ ⁡ ( Y ) = v \theta(Y)=v is well typed in Γ \Gamma .
( P P -EqSMT-BF )
We have:
Γ ⊢ c 𝖼 ′ 𝖲𝖬𝖳 : τ i → → τ Γ ⊢ Y , τ ⊳ Γ X i → ⊈ Γ Γ ⊢ X i → , τ i → ⊳ Γ ′ \begin{array}[]{c}\Gamma\vdash c^{\mathsf{SMT}}_{\mathsf{c^{\prime}}}:\vec{\tau_{i}}\rightarrow\tau\quad\quad\Gamma\vdash Y,\tau\rhd\Gamma\quad\quad\vec{X_{i}}\nsubseteq\Gamma\quad\quad\Gamma\vdash\vec{X_{i}},\vec{\tau_{i}}\rhd\Gamma^{\prime}\end{array}
The only rule that could have applied is EqSMT , where
θ ⊢ Y ∼ c 𝖼 ′ 𝖲𝖬𝖳 ​ ( X i → ) : θ ⊥ ′ \theta\vdash Y\sim c^{\mathsf{SMT}}_{\mathsf{c^{\prime}}}(\vec{X_{i}}):\theta^{\prime}_{\bot} . We must
show that θ ⊥ ′ = θ ′ \theta^{\prime}_{\bot}=\theta^{\prime} (i.e., it is not ⊥ \bot and that
Γ ⊧ θ ′ \Gamma\models\theta^{\prime} .
Since Γ ⊢ Y , τ ⊳ Γ \Gamma\vdash Y,\tau\rhd\Gamma , it must be the case that

⋅ ⊢ v : τ \cdot\vdash v:\tau and so Γ ⊢ v : τ \Gamma\vdash v:\tau
(Lemma D.6 ).
Only two rules could have applied to show θ ⊢ Y ​ c 𝖼 ′ 𝖲𝖬𝖳 ​ ( X i → ) : θ ⊥ ′ \theta\vdash Y~c^{\mathsf{SMT}}_{\mathsf{c^{\prime}}}(\vec{X_{i}}):\theta^{\prime}_{\bot} , noting the removal of the
unquote, since unification doesn’t care: u ​ u uu -BF (when some of
X i → \vec{X_{i}} are unbound) or u ​ u uu -BB (when all of the

applied, and so θ ⊥ ′ = θ ′ \theta^{\prime}_{\bot}=\theta^{\prime} .

In the former case, we apply u ​ u uu -BB inside, because
θ ⁡ ( Y ) = c 𝖼 ′ 𝖲𝖬𝖳 ​ ( v i → ) \theta(Y)=c^{\mathsf{SMT}}_{\mathsf{c^{\prime}}}(\vec{v_{i}}) . We have

(Lemma D.8 ).

Lemma E.3 on the assumption that
Γ ⊢ X i → , τ i → ⊳ Γ ′ \Gamma\vdash\vec{X_{i}},\vec{\tau_{i}}\rhd\Gamma^{\prime} ,
and the fact θ ⁡ ( Y ) = v \theta(Y)=v is well typed in Γ \Gamma .
( P P -Eq-FB )

Γ ⊢ e : τ Γ ⊢ Y , τ ⊳ Γ ′ \begin{array}[]{c}\Gamma\vdash e:\tau\quad\quad\Gamma\vdash Y,\tau\rhd\Gamma^{\prime}\end{array}

By Lemma E.2 , we know that EqExpr-E
cannot apply and that 𝒲 ; θ ⊢ e ⇓ e v \mathcal{W};\theta\vdash e\Downarrow_{e}v (and so

( P P -NegEq )
We have Γ ⊢ e : τ \Gamma\vdash e:\tau and Γ ⊢ Y , τ ⊳ Γ \Gamma\vdash Y,\tau\rhd\Gamma .
By Lemma E.2 , we know that
NegEqExpr-E1 cannot apply and that 𝒲 ; θ ⊢ e ⇓ e v \mathcal{W};\theta\vdash e\Downarrow_{e}v (and so Γ ⊢ v : τ \Gamma\vdash v:\tau ).
Since Γ ⊧ θ \Gamma\models\theta , it must be that case that Y ∈ dom ⁡ ( θ ) Y\in\mathrm{dom}(\theta) , and so NegAtom-E2 cannot have applied.
It remains to be seen that Γ ⊧ θ ′ \Gamma\models\theta^{\prime} —but

Lemma E.5 (Identical bindings implies containment).
If Γ ⊢ X , τ ⊳ Γ \Gamma\vdash X,\tau\rhd\Gamma , then X ∈ dom ⁡ ( Γ ) X\in\mathrm{dom}(\Gamma) .
Similarly, if Γ ⊢ X i → , τ i → ⊳ Γ \Gamma\vdash\vec{X_{i}},\vec{\tau_{i}}\rhd\Gamma ,
then X i → ⊆ dom ⁡ ( Γ ) \vec{X_{i}}\subseteq\mathrm{dom}(\Gamma) .
Proof.
By induction on the derivation.
( X ​ τ X\tau -Bind )
Contradictory: this rule could not have
applied, since Γ ≠ Γ , X : τ \Gamma\neq\Gamma,X:\tau .
( X ​ τ X\tau -Check )
We have X ∈ dom ⁡ ( Γ ) X\in\mathrm{dom}(\Gamma) by
assumption.
( X → ​ τ → \vec{X}\vec{\tau} -All )
By the IH on each of our
premises.
∎
Theorem E.6 (Program safety).
If Δ ; Φ ⊢ F i → ​ H j → \Delta;\Phi\vdash\vec{F_{i}}~\vec{H_{j}} and Δ ; Φ ⊧ 𝒲 \Delta;\Phi\models\mathcal{W} then
for all H ∈ H j → , ¬ ( F i → ; 𝒲 ⊢ H → ⊥ ) H\in\vec{H_{j}},~\neg(\vec{F_{i}};\mathcal{W}\vdash H\rightarrow\bot) .
Proof.
The program prog = F i → ​ H j → \mathrm{prog}=\vec{F_{i}}~\vec{H_{j}} must have been well
typed according to prog \mathrm{prog} -WF , and so we have ⊢ Δ \vdash\Delta
and ⊢ Φ \vdash\Phi along with derivations for each F F and H H :
Δ ; Φ ⊢ F 0 … Δ ; Φ ⊢ F i … Δ ; Φ ⊢ F n Δ ; Φ ⊢ H 0 … Δ ; Φ ⊢ H j … Δ ; Φ ⊢ H m \begin{array}[]{c}\Delta;\Phi\vdash F_{0}\quad\quad\dots\quad\quad\Delta;\Phi\vdash F_{i}\quad\quad\dots\quad\quad\Delta;\Phi\vdash F_{n}\\
\Delta;\Phi\vdash H_{0}\quad\quad\dots\quad\quad\Delta;\Phi\vdash H_{j}\quad\quad\dots\quad\quad\Delta;\Phi\vdash H_{m}\\
\end{array}
Let an H = p ⁡ ( X k ) : ​ - P ℓ → ∈ H j → H=p(X_{k})\mathrel{\mathsf{\mathord{:}\mathord{-}}}\vec{P_{\ell}}\in\vec{H_{j}} be given.
We know that Δ ; Φ ⊢ H \Delta;\Phi\vdash H by H H -Clause , i.e.:
⋅ ⊢ P 0 ⊳ Γ 1 … Γ ℓ ⊢ P ℓ ⊳ Γ ℓ + 1 … Γ p ⊢ P p ⊳ Γ ′ p ⊆ τ k → ∈ Φ Γ ′ ⊢ X k → , τ k → ⊳ Γ ′ \begin{array}[]{c}\cdot\vdash P_{0}\rhd\Gamma_{1}\quad\quad\dots\quad\quad\Gamma_{\ell}\vdash P_{\ell}\rhd\Gamma_{\ell+1}\quad\quad\dots\quad\quad\Gamma_{p}\vdash P_{p}\rhd\Gamma^{\prime}\\
p\subseteq\vec{\tau_{k}}\in\Phi\quad\quad\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash\vec{X_{k}},\vec{\tau_{k}}\rhd\Gamma^{\prime}\end{array}
Let 𝒲 \mathcal{W} be given such that Δ ; Φ ⊧ 𝒲 \Delta;\Phi\models\mathcal{W} .
We must show that it is not the case that F i → ; 𝒲 ⊢ H → ⊥ \vec{F_{i}};\mathcal{W}\vdash H\rightarrow\bot , i.e., Clause-E1 and Clause-E2 cannot
apply.
We can rule out Clause-E1 by
Lemma E.4 ( 1 : it
is not the case that a typesafe premise steps to ⊥ \bot .
To rule out Clause-E2 , we need to know that if we can build a
final substitution, i.e.:
⋅ ⊢ P 0 → θ 1 … θ ℓ ⊢ P ℓ → θ ℓ + 1 … θ p ⊢ P p → θ \begin{array}[]{c}\cdot\vdash P_{0}\rightarrow\theta_{1}\quad\dots\quad\theta_{\ell}\vdash P_{\ell}\rightarrow\theta_{\ell+1}\quad\dots\quad\theta_{p}\vdash P_{p}\rightarrow\theta\end{array}
then X k → ∈ dom ⁡ ( θ ) \vec{X_{k}}\in\mathrm{dom}(\theta) . We know that
X k → ⊆ dom ⁡ ( Γ ′ ) \vec{X_{k}}\subseteq\mathrm{dom}(\Gamma^{\prime}) by Lemma E.5
on Γ ′ ⊢ X k → , τ k → ⊳ Γ ′ \Gamma^{\prime}\vdash\vec{X_{k}},\vec{\tau_{k}}\rhd\Gamma^{\prime} ; since
Γ p ⊢ P p ⊳ Γ ′ \Gamma_{p}\vdash P_{p}\rhd\Gamma^{\prime} , we know by
Lemma E.4 ( 2 ) that
Γ ′ ⊧ θ \Gamma^{\prime}\models\theta . We can therefore conclude that ∀ X ∈ dom ⁡ ( Γ ′ ) , X ∈ dom ⁡ ( θ ) \forall X\in\mathrm{dom}(\Gamma^{\prime}),~X\in\mathrm{dom}(\theta) , and so
X k → ∈ dom ⁡ ( θ ) \vec{X_{k}}\in\mathrm{dom}(\theta) … and Clause-E2 cannot apply.
∎
Theorem E.7 (Program preservation).
If Δ ; Φ ⊢ F i → ​ H j → \Delta;\Phi\vdash\vec{F_{i}}~\vec{H_{j}} and Δ ; Φ ⊧ 𝒲 \Delta;\Phi\models\mathcal{W} and

Δ ; Φ ⊧ 𝒲 ′ \Delta;\Phi\models\mathcal{W}^{\prime} .
Proof.
The program prog = F i → ​ H j → \mathrm{prog}=\vec{F_{i}}~\vec{H_{j}} must have been well

Appendix F Model-theoretic semantics
We have focused on the operational semantics of Formulog, as it helps us to
reason about type safety.
However, since we have kept Formulog close to Datalog, it is also possible to give
a model-theoretic semantics to a Formulog program.
First, all ML functions and expressions are desugared into Datalog rules; this
translation is relatively straightforward, with the trickiest part being the
translation of non-mutually exclusive patterns occurring in match expressions.
For each primitive operator, we introduce a (possibly infinite) EDB relation
that defines that operator; for example, the addition operator   + is
represented through a ternary relation add ( x x , y y , z z ) , which states
that z z is the sum of x x and y y .
Terms of the form p ⁡ ( w ∗ ) p(w^{*}) (i.e., invocations of predicates as functions) are
translated to aggregate predicates.
Formulog requires the use of these terms, as well as negation, to be stratified;
thus, the program resulting from the translation can be given a perfect model
semantics in line with stratified
negation ( Apt
et al., 1988 ; Przymusinski, 1988 ; Van Gelder, 1989 )
and stratified aggregation ( Mumick
et al., 1990 ) .
For a small example, consider this Formulog program:
⬇
fun length ( Xs : ’ a list ) : bv [32] =
match Xs with
| [] => 0
| _ :: T => 1 + length ( T )
end
ok :- length ([1, 2, 3]) = 3.
This would be translated into a program like this:
⬇
length ([], 0).
length ( _ :: T , Z ) :-
length ( T , L ),
add (1, L , Z ).
ok :- length ([1, 2, 3], 3).
Note that the rules defining the length predicate violate the range
restriction, and in fact define an infinite relation.
This does not pose a fundamental problem to the model theory.
To make this program evaluable, we could rewrite the length predicate’s
definition (and its uses) via the magic set transformation; the resulting
relations would meet the range restriction.
While the magic set transformation can turn a stratified program into a
non-stratified program, there are techniques to either restore
stratification ( Meskes and Noack, 1993 ) or correctly evaluate the
non-stratified program ( Mumick
et al., 1990 ; Balbin et al., 1991 ) .
